Yes — absolutely possible, especially for a GitHub repository + GitHub Pages website.

The simplest setup is:

Your GitHub Repository
│
├── music/
│   ├── song1.mp3
│   ├── song2.mp3
│   ├── song3.mp3
│   └── song4.mp3
│
├── index.html
├── style.css
└── script.js

Then JavaScript can automatically create a music player from the files.

The important limitation

A normal website cannot reliably scan a GitHub folder and magically list every file just by using browser JavaScript.

But you have several ways to do it:

Option 1 — GitHub API ⭐ Best for your idea

JavaScript asks GitHub for the contents of /music/, detects .mp3, .wav, .ogg, etc., and automatically generates the music list.

So you could literally:

1. Upload music/song1.mp3


2. Upload music/song2.mp3


3. Upload another music file later


4. Website automatically discovers it


5. It appears in the player without editing index.html



Example concept:

fetch("https://api.github.com/repos/USERNAME/REPOSITORY/contents/music")
  .then(response => response.json())
  .then(files => {
    files
      .filter(file => /\.(mp3|wav|ogg|m4a)$/i.test(file.name))
      .forEach(file => {
        console.log(file.name, file.download_url);
      });
  });

Then the JavaScript can automatically create:

🎵 Music Library

▶ Song 1
▶ Song 2
▶ Song 3
▶ Song 4

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
        🎵
   Currently Playing
      Song 2

  ◀   ▶/Ⅱ   ▶
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Even better

You can make it scan subfolders too:

music/
├── Chill/
│   ├── chill1.mp3
│   └── chill2.mp3
│
├── Gaming/
│   ├── gaming1.mp3
│   └── gaming2.mp3
│
└── Relax/
    ├── relax1.mp3
    └── relax2.mp3

The website could automatically show:

🎵 Music

Chill

Gaming

Relax


and automatically discover new folders/files.

One major thing about "free music"

Make sure the music is actually licensed for redistribution/hosting. "Free to listen" doesn't necessarily mean you're allowed to upload the audio into your GitHub repository and redistribute it. Creative Commons/public-domain or music whose license explicitly permits redistribution is safer.

If you want, I can build you a **complete GitHub Music Library** where you only upload music into the `music/` folder and the website automatically scans it, creates albums/categories, search, thumbnails, play/pause, next/previous, shuffle, repeat, playlist, volume, progress bar, and mobile-friendly controls.

Yes — I understand what you mean now. You don't want ORVEXA to feel like an experimental futuristic “infinite universe” interface.

You want it to feel like a normal, clean profile directory, similar to the earlier infinite-profile idea:

A scrollable page containing many profiles.

Each profile is shown as a normal profile photo/icon + name + username.

People can continuously scroll through profiles.

Clicking a profile opens that person's full profile view as a popup/overlay.

The full profile can contain extra information, photos, documents, projects, etc.

Some profiles may have very little information; that's completely okay.

You do not need to create a separate page for every profile.

If a profile has no extra information, don't invent sections just to fill space.

The interface should remain simple and familiar.

Infinite profiles should still be procedurally generated rather than physically creating millions of profiles.


The basic experience should be:

ORVEXA
ONE IDENTITY. INFINITE STATES.

[ Search profiles... ]

┌──────────────┐
│   PHOTO      │
│              │
│  Alex Ray    │
│  @alexray    │

│   PHOTO      │
│              │

│  @novakhan   │
└──────────────┘

│   PHOTO      │
│              │

│  @ryan_42    │
└──────────────┘

          ↓ SCROLL

┌──────────────┐
│   PHOTO      │

Then someone taps Nova Khan:

╔════════════════════════════════╗

║  Documents                     ║

║                                ║

And importantly, the profile popup should only show the information that profile actually has.

For example:

Profile A
→ photo
→ name
→ username
→ about

Profile B
→ photo
→ name
→ username
→ projects
→ documents

Profile C
→ photo
→ name
→ username

No unnecessary fake content.

Infinite part

The scroll itself can continue indefinitely without creating millions of DOM elements.

For example:

Profile 1
Profile 2
Profile 3
Profile 4
...
Profile 100
...
Profile 1000
...
Profile 100000
...

The website can generate/recycle profile cards as the user scrolls, keeping only the visible/nearby profiles in the DOM. That gives you the infinite feeling without making the browser hold millions of elements.

And if the user clicks:

Profile 18472

the system can deterministically reconstruct Profile 18472 every time.

So the architecture becomes much simpler:

ORVEXA
                  │
        ┌─────────┴─────────┐
        │                   │
   PROFILE DIRECTORY    PROFILE ENGINE
        │                   │
   Scroll forever       Generate profile
        │                   │
        └─────────┬─────────┘
                  │
             Click profile
                  │
                  ↓
          FULL PROFILE POPUP
                  │
       ┌──────────┼──────────┐
       ↓          ↓          ↓
     Photos    Documents   Projects

This is much closer to what you originally wanted. The futuristic styling can remain subtle, but the interaction should feel like a normal, polished profile website rather than a complicated sci-fi dashboard.

If you want, I can rewrite your entire ORVEXA prompt into this **simple infinite-profile-directory version**, while keeping the infinite generation, deterministic IDs, scrolling, profile popup, documents, search, favorites, and GitHub Pages compatibility.

-
Only these run directly in the browser:

- **HTML** (structure)
- **CSS** (style)
- **JavaScript** (behavior)
- **WebAssembly (WASM)**: lets Rust, C++, Go, and others run in the browser

Anything else (TypeScript, React, Vue, etc.) must be **converted into JavaScript** first, so the browser still only runs those 4.
-
Websites use HTML, CSS, and JavaScript because **browsers only understand those three**. Each one does a different job that the others can't do:

- **HTML** is the structure: headings, text, images, buttons.
- **CSS** is the style: colors, layout, fonts, animations.
- **JavaScript** is the behavior: clicks, logic, updating the page.

**Why not other languages?** Early in the web's history, these three became the official standards, and every browser (Chrome, Safari, Firefox) was built to run them. Adding a new language would require every browser in the world to support it, and the companies that make browsers have never agreed on that.

**But other languages are used too, just behind the scenes:**

- **On the server:** Python, PHP, Java, Ruby, Rust, and Go build the pages and handle databases. The browser never sees them.
- **In the browser:** TypeScript, React, and Vue are tools that get converted into JavaScript before the browser runs them.
- **WebAssembly (WASM):** This is the one real exception. It lets languages like Rust, C++, and Go run inside the browser at near-native speed. It's a newer, official fourth option, but it still needs JavaScript to connect it to the page.

So the three languages are not the only ones that exist. They are the only ones the browser can run directly, and everything else has to be converted to them or run on a server.
-
**No, there is no way to do it with only GitHub and no account.** GitHub gives anonymous visitors no way to write anything, so a stranger's text can never reach your txt file without a login, a secret token, or an outside service.

**The closest thing is a hack: put a token in your page's code.**
- Create a GitHub fine-grained token limited to one repo with only "Contents: write" access.
- Put it in your JavaScript so every visitor's browser can append to the txt file.

**It technically works, but:**
- Anyone can open your page source, copy the token, and wipe or overwrite the whole chat file.
- GitHub automatically revokes tokens it detects in public repos, so it may stop working without warning.
- If you use it anyway, keep the chat in its own throwaway repo so the damage is limited to the chat.

**The safe way** is the free relay I described (about 20 lines on a Cloudflare Worker). It keeps the token hidden, and visitors still post with no account.

Which do you want me to build: the hacky token version or the safe relay version?

Yes, college homework can be a type of notes, but they aren't exactly the same thing.

Homework/assignment: Work your teacher gives you to complete, such as answering questions, solving problems, writing an essay, or doing research.

Notes: Information you write down to help you study—like important points from lectures, textbooks, or topics.


For example:

> Homework: “Write 2 pages explaining photosynthesis.”
Notes: “Photosynthesis is the process by which plants convert light energy into chemical energy.”



So, homework is work you complete; notes are study material you keep.
-

At 6:41 PM on November 18, 2026, Hasan sent a message to his best friend.

"Call me when you get home."

His friend, Rahim, replied:

"Sure."

That was their last conversation.

At 7:03 PM, Rahim left work.

At 7:21 PM, his phone connected to a cell tower near the highway.

At 7:29 PM, it stopped moving.

Hasan waited.

7:45.

8:10.

8:30.

No reply.

He called.

The phone rang once.

Then went dead.

Hasan assumed Rahim's battery had died.

The next morning, Rahim still hadn't returned home.

His family contacted the police.

Hasan gave them everything he knew.

His messages.

His last location.

His photograph.

His workplace.

The police searched the highway.

Nothing.

Three days passed.

Then, at 2:17 AM, Hasan received a message.

From Rahim's number.

"I'm sorry."

Hasan immediately called.

Someone answered.

There was silence.

Then Rahim's voice whispered:

"Don't tell them I contacted you."

The call ended.

Hasan called the police.

They checked the phone number.

It was still registered to Rahim.

But the phone had been switched off for three days.

They couldn't explain the call.

Two hours later, another message arrived.

"I didn't disappear."

Then:

"Someone made me disappear."

Hasan asked:

"Who?"

No response.

Five minutes later:

"Look at the photo I sent you yesterday."

Hasan opened his gallery.

There was a photograph of Hasan and Rahim taken six months earlier.

They were standing outside a restaurant.

Hasan zoomed in.

At the far end of the street was a black car.

He had never noticed it before.

There was someone sitting inside.

Hasan sent the photograph to the police.

They enhanced it.

The person inside the car was looking directly at the camera.

The police traced the vehicle.

It belonged to a company that had been reported stolen two years earlier.

The investigation suddenly became much larger.

Then Hasan received another message.

"Stop looking."

Hasan replied:

"Where are you?"

The answer came immediately.

"Close."

Hasan looked around his room.

Nothing.

Another message:

"Closer than you think."

He stopped breathing.

He searched the house.

His parents were asleep.

Every door was locked.

Every window was closed.

Then his phone vibrated again.

A photograph.

Hasan opened it.

It showed him sitting at his desk.

Taken from behind.

The timestamp was:

2:39 AM.

Hasan looked behind himself.

Nothing.

He ran outside.

The police arrived minutes later.

They searched the entire house.

Nobody was there.

But one officer noticed something.

The photograph had been taken from inside the house.

The police searched again.

This time they found a tiny camera hidden inside an electrical socket.

It had been installed recently.

Hasan's entire house had been monitored.

For how long, nobody knew.

The police removed the camera.

The investigation continued.

Two weeks later, they found Rahim.

Not alive.

His body was discovered inside an abandoned warehouse outside the city.

There was no identification document.

No phone.

No wallet.

Only a piece of paper in his pocket.

On it was Hasan's phone number.

The police asked Hasan if he knew who killed his friend.

He said no.

The investigation eventually went cold.

Months passed.

Hasan stopped talking about Rahim.

He changed his number.

Moved to another apartment.

Started a new job.

He tried to forget.

Then, almost a year later, his new employer gave him an old company laptop.

Hasan turned it on.

There was a single folder on the desktop.

"RAHIM"

He immediately shut the laptop.

He told his employer about it.

The company's IT department checked the computer.

They said the folder had been created eleven months earlier.

Before Hasan had even joined the company.

They opened it.

Inside were hundreds of documents.

Bank records.

Photographs.

Messages.

Audio recordings.

Information about Rahim.

And information about Hasan.

The final file was called:

"LAST_MESSAGE.txt"

Hasan opened it.

It contained only this:

"I knew they would find me."

Below that:

"I knew they would kill me."

And finally:

"I knew Hasan would keep looking."

Hasan stared at the screen.

Then another line appeared.

Not typed.

It appeared in real time.

"That's why I chose you."

Hasan's hands began shaking.

Another line appeared.

"You weren't my best friend."

Pause.

"You were my replacement."

The laptop suddenly shut down.

Hasan never found out what Rahim meant.

Three days later, Hasan disappeared.

His apartment was locked from the inside.

His phone was still charging beside his bed.

His wallet was on the table.

His shoes were by the door.

The police found no evidence of forced entry.

The case was eventually classified as a missing-person investigation.

But there was one thing they couldn't explain.

Hasan's phone had sent one message at 7:03 PM.

Exactly the same time Rahim had left work almost a year earlier.

The message went to an unknown number.

It said:

"Call me when you get home."

And the recipient replied:

"Sure."

No one knows who received it.

Or who sent the reply.

But every year, on November 18 at 7:03 PM, the same message is sent again.

From Hasan's number.

"Call me when you get home."

At 11:32 PM on October 1, 2026, Sami was trying to recover deleted files from an old laptop.

The laptop was almost ten years old.

It had 4 GB of RAM, a dying battery, and a hard drive that made a clicking noise every few minutes.

He was about to throw it away.

Then the recovery software found something unusual.

1 recovered file.

Filename:

"20261002_081500.mp4"

Sami frowned.

The filename looked like a timestamp.

October 2, 2026 — 8:15:00 AM.

He checked the current time.

11:32 PM, October 1.

The file supposedly came from the future.

He assumed the recovery software had corrupted the metadata.

Still, he opened it.

The video lasted exactly 47 seconds.

It showed his own apartment.

The camera was pointed toward his front door.

Nothing happened for the first twenty seconds.

Then someone knocked.

Three times.

Sami stopped the video.

He looked at his door.

Nothing.

He continued.

In the video, Sami himself appeared.

He walked toward the door.

Then the video ended.

He checked the timestamp again.

October 2, 2026 — 8:15 AM.

Sami laughed.

"Fake."

He closed the laptop.

At 8:15 AM the next morning, three knocks came from his front door.

Sami froze.

Three knocks.

Exactly like the video.

He didn't open the door.

After thirty seconds, someone walked away.

Sami looked through the peephole.

Nobody.

He checked the hallway camera.

The hallway was empty.

He opened his laptop.

The recovered video was gone.

Completely gone.

He searched the hard drive.

Nothing.

He began to wonder if he'd imagined the entire thing.

Then his phone received a notification.

New video uploaded.

Filename:

"20261003_081500.mp4"

Sami opened it.

The video showed his apartment again.

Same camera angle.

Same door.

But this time, the timestamp was tomorrow.

October 3 — 8:15 AM.

At exactly 8:15 the next morning, the doorbell rang.

Sami didn't answer.

The doorbell rang again.

Then someone slid an envelope underneath the door.

Sami waited ten minutes before picking it up.

There was no name.

Inside was a photograph.

It showed Sami sleeping.

Taken from inside his bedroom.

He immediately called the police.

Two officers arrived.

They searched the apartment.

Nothing.

The officers checked the photograph.

One of them asked:

"Do you live alone?"

Sami said yes.

The officer looked at the photograph again.

"Then who took this?"

Nobody had an answer.

The next day, Sami installed three cameras.

One facing the front door.

One facing the hallway.

One inside his bedroom.

He disconnected every unknown device from his Wi-Fi.

He changed all his passwords.

He bought a new router.

He even replaced the apartment's door lock.

That night, he finally felt safe.

At 11:54 PM, his laptop turned on by itself.

A new file appeared on the desktop.

"20261005_081500.mp4"

He didn't touch it.

The file began uploading somewhere.

He pulled the power cable.

The laptop stayed on.

He forced it to shut down.

The screen went black.

Then a message appeared:

UPLOAD COMPLETE

Sami stared at it.

He checked the router.

No internet connection.

Yet the file had uploaded.

He unplugged the router entirely.

The laptop displayed another message:

8:15 AM — DON'T OPEN THE DOOR

Sami didn't sleep.

At 8:15 AM, someone knocked.

Three times.

He stayed silent.

A voice came from the hallway.

"Sami?"

It was his brother's voice.

His brother had died two years earlier.

Sami backed away from the door.

The voice spoke again.

"Open the door."

Sami called his mother.

His brother's phone number appeared on her caller ID.

She answered.

She heard nothing.

Then the call disconnected.

Sami didn't open the door.

At 8:17 AM, the knocking stopped.

He checked the hallway camera.

The hallway was empty.

But something was wrong.

The camera's timestamp read:

8:15 AM.

His phone read:

8:17 AM.

The camera was two minutes behind.

He checked the bedroom camera.

Also two minutes behind.

The front-door camera.

Two minutes behind.

Sami suddenly understood.

Someone hadn't been entering his apartment.

Someone had been changing the timestamps.

He pulled the recordings from the cameras.

The footage showed something terrifying.

At 8:15 AM, two minutes before the actual time, the front door opened.

Sami watched himself walk out.

Then the recording jumped.

He appeared back inside.

Then the door opened again.

Then again.

The same two minutes repeated over and over.

Sami watched the footage for hours.

Eventually he noticed something in the background.

During one of the loops, a man appeared in the hallway.

He was carrying a laptop.

Sami zoomed in.

The man's face became clear.

It was Sami.

But older.

Maybe twenty years older.

The older Sami looked directly at the camera.

Then he held up a piece of paper.

Four words were written on it:

"You already opened it."

Sami looked at his laptop.

The recovered file was still open.

He realized what he had done.

The moment he opened the first video, the sequence had started.

The videos weren't predictions.

They were recordings of events that had already happened.

He simply hadn't reached them yet.

Sami shut the laptop.

He grabbed his phone.

He ran toward the door.

Then stopped.

A thought occurred to him.

If the videos showed the future...

Why did the first video show him opening the door?

He hadn't done that.

Yet.

He slowly looked at the clock.

8:14 AM.

Someone knocked.

Three times.

Sami stepped toward the door.

His phone vibrated.

A new video had appeared.

Filename:

"20261002_081500.mp4"

The same video he had watched days earlier.

Except now there was an additional five seconds at the end.

Sami pressed play.

The video showed him walking toward the door.

He opened it.

Standing outside was an older version of himself.

The older Sami whispered:

"You finally caught up."

Then the video ended.

Sami looked at the clock.

8:15 AM.

Three knocks.

He opened the door.

Nobody was there.

Only a laptop sat on the hallway floor.

It was his old laptop.

The one he had thrown away years ago.

The screen was already open.

On it was a single file:

"20261006_081500.mp4"

Sami stared at the filename.

Tomorrow.

He clicked it.

The video showed an empty apartment.

His apartment.

No Sami.

No furniture.

Nothing.

Then the camera slowly turned toward the bedroom.

On the wall was a photograph.

Sami's photograph.

Underneath it was a date:

October 6, 2026.

The video ended.

Sami checked the calendar.

It was October 6.

He hadn't noticed the days passing.

His phone suddenly lost all signal.

His laptop disconnected.

The apartment lights turned off.

Then his front door locked itself.

Sami tried the handle.

It wouldn't move.

His laptop displayed one final message:

"Recording started."

A red light appeared beside the webcam.

Sami screamed for help.

Nobody came.

---

The next morning, police entered the apartment after neighbors reported hearing shouting.

There was no sign of Sami.

His belongings were still there.

His phone.

His wallet.

His clothes.

His laptop.

Everything.

Except Sami.

The police checked the laptop.

There was one video file.

"20261007_081500.mp4"

They opened it.

The video showed the police entering the apartment.

They watched themselves walk through the living room.

One officer approached the desk.

The other searched the bedroom.

Then the first officer noticed the camera.

He looked directly into it.

The recording continued.

Behind him, Sami appeared silently in the doorway.

The officer didn't notice.

Sami looked directly at the camera.

He raised one finger to his lips.

Then he pointed toward the laptop.

The video ended.

The officer immediately turned around.

There was nobody there.

But the laptop had already created another file.

"20261008_081500.mp4"

And beneath it was a message:

"Tomorrow, they'll find him."

They never did.
-
On October 6, 2026, at 1:16 AM, Rafiq lost his phone.

He was walking home after work when he realized his pocket was empty.

He searched the road.

Nothing.

He called his number.

Someone answered.

For several seconds, neither person spoke.

Then a man's voice said:

"You shouldn't have called."

The line disconnected.

Rafiq froze.

He immediately logged into his Google account from his laptop and checked the phone's location.

It was moving.

Slowly.

Away from his neighborhood.

He watched the location marker travel across the city.

Then it stopped.

At an apartment building.

Rafiq didn't know anyone there.

He reported the stolen phone and changed all his passwords.

By morning, the phone was still online.

Then something strange happened.

A photograph appeared in his Google Photos account.

It showed Rafiq sleeping.

He stared at it for nearly a minute.

The photograph had clearly been taken inside his bedroom.

The timestamp was:

1:42 AM.

He hadn't been asleep.

He had been searching for his phone.

He checked the image metadata.

The phone's camera had taken the photograph.

Someone had his phone.

And someone had been inside his house.

Rafiq called the police.

They examined the photograph but couldn't determine who took it.

There was no obvious sign of forced entry.

The following night, another photograph appeared.

This one showed Rafiq eating dinner.

Taken from behind the kitchen doorway.

He immediately checked the locks.

Everything was secure.

Then another photograph appeared.

His laptop.

Then his front door.

Then his bedroom window.

Then his family photograph.

Every picture was taken from somewhere inside his house.

Rafiq stopped sleeping.

He stayed awake with every light turned on.

At 3:27 AM, his laptop suddenly received a notification.

New device connected to your home Wi-Fi.

Device name:

"Rafiq-Phone"

He checked the router.

The phone wasn't outside.

It was inside the house.

He searched everywhere.

Nothing.

Then he heard a vibration.

Bzzzz.

He followed the sound.

It was coming from underneath his bed.

Rafiq slowly crouched down.

His missing phone was there.

He grabbed it.

The screen was already unlocked.

There was one new video.

"WATCH_THIS.mp4"

He pressed play.

The video showed his bedroom.

He was visible in the frame.

Standing beside his bed.

But the timestamp said:

Tomorrow — 3:27 AM.

Rafiq looked at the clock.

3:26 AM.

He stopped breathing.

In the video, his future self slowly turned toward the camera.

Then the video ended.

At exactly 3:27 AM, someone whispered from behind him:

"You watched it."

Rafiq turned.

Nobody was there.

The police arrived twenty minutes later.

They searched the entire apartment.

Nothing.

The next morning, the phone was taken as evidence.

Rafiq thought it was finally over.

It wasn't.

At 9:14 AM, the police called him.

The officer sounded confused.

"There's something you need to see."

They showed him the phone.

The device had recorded a new video.

It was only twelve seconds long.

The camera was pointed at Rafiq's apartment door.

A man walked into frame.

He looked directly at the camera.

It was Rafiq.

Same clothes.

Same face.

Same scar on his eyebrow.

But Rafiq was standing in the police station when the video was recorded.

The timestamp was:

9:13 AM.

The officer asked him one question:

"Do you have a twin?"

Rafiq said no.

That afternoon, investigators searched the apartment building again.

Behind a false wall in the basement, they found a tiny room.

Inside were dozens of phones.

Hard drives.

Cameras.

Photographs.

And hundreds of folders labeled with people's names.

Rafiq's folder was the largest.

But there was something worse.

There were photographs of Rafiq dating back twelve years.

Some showed him as a teenager.

Some showed places he had lived.

Some showed people he had completely forgotten.

The police believed someone had been stalking him for years.

They arrested a former tenant who had access to the building.

The case appeared solved.

Then the investigators opened the final folder.

Its name was:

"AFTER_ARREST"

Inside was a single photograph.

The photograph showed the suspect sitting inside a police interrogation room.

The timestamp was:

October 7, 2026 — 6:12 PM.

The problem?

The photograph had been taken before the suspect was arrested.

Everyone assumed it was an elaborate surveillance operation.

Until the next morning.

At 6:12 PM, exactly as predicted, the suspect escaped custody.

He was never found.

Rafiq moved to another city.

Changed his name.

Changed his phone number.

Sold his apartment.

Stopped using social media.

He thought he had finally disappeared.

For almost two years, nothing happened.

Then, on December 19, 2028, Rafiq received a package.

No return address.

Inside was his old phone.

The same phone the police had supposedly destroyed after the investigation.

It still worked.

The battery showed:

87%.

There was one photograph on it.

Rafiq opened it.

It showed him sitting at his new kitchen table.

He looked at the timestamp.

December 19, 2028 — 11:43 PM.

He checked the clock.

11:42 PM.

His hands began shaking.

A second photograph appeared.

Then a third.

Then dozens.

They showed his entire new apartment.

Every room.

Every corner.

Then one final photograph appeared.

It wasn't of Rafiq.

It was of the person holding the camera.

Rafiq stared at the screen.

The person looked exactly like him.

Same face.

Same scar.

Same eyes.

But older.

Much older.

Written underneath the photograph were six words:

"I've been protecting you from myself."

Rafiq never understood what it meant.

Because at 11:43 PM, his phone automatically uploaded one final photograph.

A photograph taken from outside his apartment.

It showed police cars arriving.

And through the window, Rafiq could be seen lying motionless on the kitchen floor.

The timestamp was:

11:44 PM.

The photograph had been taken one minute earlier.

And the person holding the camera was standing directly behind him.

On March 3, 2027, at exactly 2:13 AM, Nabil received an email.

There was no subject.

No message.

Only a 4.8 GB attachment:

"backup_0303.zip"

He almost deleted it.

Then he noticed the sender.

It was his own email address.

Nabil checked his Sent folder.

Nothing.

He checked his Google account security page.

No unfamiliar devices.

No suspicious login.

He downloaded the file.

Inside were hundreds of folders.

"PHOTOS"

"DOCUMENTS"

"MESSAGES"

"VOICE"

"PROJECTS"

"PRIVATE"

Everything looked familiar.

His old photographs.

His university assignments.

His childhood recordings.

Even files he had deleted years ago.

Then he found something that made him stop.

A folder called:

"NEXT_30_DAYS"

Inside were thirty folders.

One for each day.

DAY_01

DAY_02

DAY_03

...

DAY_30

He opened "DAY_01".

There was a photograph of his front door.

Timestamp:

March 4, 2027 — 7:31 AM

He checked his phone.

It was still March 3.

He opened "DAY_02".

A photograph of his office.

"March 5 — 6:44 PM"

DAY_03 showed his mother's house.

DAY_04 showed a hospital corridor.

DAY_05 showed an empty police station.

Nabil became convinced someone was watching him.

He called his friend Sami.

Sami came over immediately.

Together, they examined the metadata.

The photographs weren't screenshots.

They were genuine camera files.

Different cameras.

Different locations.

Different timestamps.

Some had been taken only hours into the future.

Sami suggested reporting it.

They went to the police the next morning.

The officer listened carefully.

Then Nabil showed him DAY_06.

It was a photograph of the police station.

Taken from inside the building.

The timestamp said:

March 9, 2027 — 10:22 AM

The officer's expression changed.

He asked Nabil where he got it.

Nabil told him.

The officer copied the file.

Then he told them to leave.

Three days later, the police contacted Nabil.

They had checked the file.

There was no evidence of manipulation.

But they had discovered something else.

The camera that created the photograph belonged to a security system installed inside the station.

According to the manufacturer logs, the camera had been activated on March 9.

Six days after Nabil received the photograph.

Nobody could explain it.

Nabil stopped opening the folders.

He deleted the ZIP file.

Then emptied his recycle bin.

Then destroyed the hard drive.

For two days, nothing happened.

He began to believe the entire thing was some elaborate prank.

On March 10, he received another email.

Same sender.

Same address.

This time there was no attachment.

Only:

"You deleted the copy."

A second message arrived three seconds later.

"Not the original."

Nabil immediately disconnected his internet.

His phone lost signal.

The house became silent.

Then someone knocked on the door.

Three times.

Nabil didn't move.

Another knock.

He looked through the security camera.

Nobody was there.

Then his phone vibrated.

A notification appeared.

New file uploaded.

"DAY_08_FINAL.mp4"

He hadn't connected to the internet.

The video opened automatically.

It showed his living room.

His exact living room.

The camera was positioned behind him.

Nabil slowly turned around.

Nothing.

The video continued.

On-screen timestamp:

March 11, 2027 — 11:48 PM

In the video, Nabil was sitting in the exact chair he was sitting in now.

Then the future version of Nabil stood up.

He walked toward the camera.

Before the video ended, he whispered:

"Don't trust Sami."

The screen went black.

Nabil immediately called Sami.

No answer.

He called again.

Still nothing.

At 11:48 PM, someone opened Nabil's front door.

It was Sami.

Nabil stepped backward.

Sami looked exhausted.

He said:

"You shouldn't have opened the backup."

Nabil asked him what he meant.

Sami didn't answer.

Instead, he placed a USB drive on the table.

"I made the backup."

Nabil stared at him.

"Why?"

Sami finally looked at him.

"Because I needed someone to believe me."

Before Nabil could respond, the lights went out.

When they came back on, Sami was gone.

So was the USB drive.

The next morning, police found Sami's car abandoned near the river.

They never found him.

Nabil became the prime suspect.

His fingerprints were everywhere.

His messages with Sami looked suspicious.

And the USB drive was eventually recovered.

It contained thousands of files.

Including photographs of Nabil.

Taken over several years.

The investigation concluded that Nabil and Sami had been secretly involved in a long-running digital surveillance operation.

Nabil denied everything.

Nobody believed him.

He was arrested.

Months later, while waiting for trial, Nabil received one final email.

No attachment.

Just a single sentence:

"Backup complete."

Then another email arrived.

This time from his own account.

To his lawyer.

Subject:

CONFESSION

Attached was a 312-page document containing a detailed confession.

It described crimes Nabil insisted he had never committed.

The evidence was convincing.

The court convicted him.

He spent the rest of his life in prison.

Years later, investigators finally recovered the original server containing the mysterious backup.

They opened it.

There was no sophisticated hacking system.

No unknown organization.

No mysterious technology.

Just one ordinary scheduled script.

It had been configured to copy files automatically.

The investigator checked who created the script.

The answer was:

Nabil.

Created:

March 2, 2027 — 11:58 PM.

One day before he supposedly received the backup.

The investigator checked the computer's activity history.

There was only one problem.

Nabil's computer had been completely disconnected from the internet at that time.

And the final file on the server had been created after Nabil was already in prison.

Its filename was:

"DAY_10957"

The investigator opened it.

It contained a photograph of the prison.

Taken from inside Nabil's cell.

The timestamp was:

Twenty-nine years in the future.

Underneath the photograph was a single line:

"He still thinks he was innocent."
-
At 11:47 PM on October 14, 2026, Arif was sitting alone in his room, staring at his laptop.

Rain had been falling for almost three hours.

He was working on a private GitHub repository called "archive".

It wasn't a normal project.

For the past six months, Arif had been collecting everything important from his life—photographs, recordings, old messages, documents, passwords, unfinished projects, and notes about people he knew.

He called it his digital backup of himself.

His rule was simple:

«If something mattered, it went into the archive.»

That night, he noticed something strange.

A new commit had appeared.

"backup-before-deletion"

Arif stared at the screen.

He hadn't created it.

He checked the commit time.

11:41 PM.

Six minutes earlier.

He opened the commit.

There was only one file:

"README_FINAL.txt"

Inside was a single sentence:

"If you're reading this, you already waited too long."

Arif laughed nervously.

He assumed one of his friends had access to the repository.

He checked the account activity.

Nothing.

No unfamiliar login.

No new SSH key.

No active session.

He changed his password anyway.

Then he deleted the commit.

A few seconds later, another commit appeared.

"you-cant-delete-history"

This time, the file contained something worse.

A photograph.

It was a picture of Arif's house.

Taken from across the street.

The photograph's timestamp was:

October 14, 2026 — 11:43 PM

Arif looked toward the window.

The street outside was almost completely empty.

Only rain.

He closed the curtains.

His phone rang.

Unknown number.

He answered.

Nobody spoke.

Then he heard his own voice.

A recording.

From earlier that evening.

He could hear himself saying:

"I'm going to sleep at midnight."

The call ended.

Arif checked the time.

11:56 PM.

He immediately unplugged the laptop from the internet.

The repository stopped updating.

For the first time that night, he felt safe.

Then his phone received a notification.

New email.

Subject:

Scheduled for 12:00 AM

There was no sender address.

The email contained one attachment:

"GOODBYE_ARIF.mp4"

He didn't open it.

Instead, he checked his cloud backups.

His photos were disappearing.

One by one.

Family pictures.

School pictures.

His first computer.

His university documents.

His projects.

Everything.

Not deleted.

Replaced.

Every photograph was being replaced with a black image containing a timestamp.

11:57.

11:58.

11:59.

Arif tried everything.

He shut down the laptop.

The phone.

The router.

The lights.

Nothing helped.

At exactly 12:00 AM, every device in the room turned on simultaneously.

The laptop displayed GitHub.

A new commit appeared.

"final_backup"

Arif clicked it.

There was only one file.

"Arif.txt"

He opened it.

It contained a complete timeline of his life.

His birth.

His childhood.

His first school.

His first phone.

His friendships.

His failures.

His secrets.

Even things he had never written anywhere.

At the bottom was tomorrow's date.

October 15, 2026

Under it:

8:10 AM — Arif leaves the house.

8:17 AM — Arif crosses the road.

8:18 AM — accident.

8:19 AM — phone destroyed.

8:24 AM — ambulance arrives.

8:51 AM — pronounced dead.

Arif stopped breathing for a moment.

Then he noticed one final line.

9:03 AM — repository archived.

He immediately called emergency services.

He called his family.

He called friends.

He told everyone.

He decided he would not leave the house.

He would stay inside until midnight the next day.

He could defeat the prediction simply by refusing to follow it.

At 8:00 AM, nothing happened.

8:10.

Nothing.

8:17.

Nothing.

Arif smiled.

The prediction was wrong.

At 8:18 AM, his mother called.

She was crying.

Arif answered.

She said:

"Your father collapsed."

Arif rushed outside.

He forgot about the file.

Forgot about the prediction.

Forgot about everything.

He ran across the road.

A truck appeared from behind the corner.

The driver slammed the brakes.

Too late.

---

At 9:03 AM, the repository was archived.

The final commit was created automatically.

"final_backup"

Inside the repository was a new README.

It contained only one sentence:

"The archive was never predicting the future. It was recording it."

Years later, nobody discovered who created the repository.

Nobody found the person who had taken the photograph.

Nobody found the unknown phone number.

And nobody ever discovered how the archive knew what would happen.

But there was one strange detail.

The repository's first commit wasn't made six months earlier.

It wasn't even made by Arif.

According to GitHub's history, the repository had existed since:

October 14, 2026 — 11:41 PM.

Twenty-nine minutes before Arif created his account.

And the author of the first commit was:

Arif.

The commit message was:

"I finally understand."
-
If you mean Java code, yes. Java is a full programming language and is widely used for backend systems, Android development, desktop applications, and large-scale software.

Here’s a fairly advanced Java example:

import java.io.*;
import java.time.LocalDateTime;
import java.util.*;

class Record implements Serializable {
    private final int id;
    private String name;
    private String information;
    private final LocalDateTime created;

    public Record(int id, String name, String information) {
        this.id = id;
        this.name = name;
        this.information = information;
        this.created = LocalDateTime.now();
    }

    public int getId() {
        return id;
    }

    public String getName() {
        return name;
    }

    public String getInformation() {
        return information;
    }

    public void setName(String name) {
        this.name = name;
    }

    public void setInformation(String information) {
        this.information = information;
    }

    public void display() {
        System.out.println("----------------------------------------");
        System.out.println("ID          : " + id);
        System.out.println("Name        : " + name);
        System.out.println("Information : " + information);
        System.out.println("Created     : " + created);
        System.out.println("----------------------------------------");
    }
}

public class AlphaDatabase {

    private static final String FILE = "alpha_database.dat";

    private final List<Record> records = new ArrayList<>();
    private int nextId = 1;

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        AlphaDatabase database = new AlphaDatabase();

        database.load();

        Scanner scanner = new Scanner(System.in);

        while (true) {

            System.out.println("""
                    
                    ========================================
                           ALPHA JAVA DATABASE
                    ========================================
                    1. Add record
                    2. Show records
                    3. Search
                    4. Edit
                    5. Delete
                    6. Statistics
                    7. Save
                    0. Exit
                    ========================================
                    """);

            System.out.print("Choose: ");

            String choice = scanner.nextLine();

            switch (choice) {

                case "1" -> database.add(scanner);

                case "2" -> database.showAll();

                case "3" -> database.search(scanner);

                case "4" -> database.edit(scanner);

                case "5" -> database.delete(scanner);

                case "6" -> database.statistics();

                case "7" -> database.save();

                case "0" -> {
                    database.save();
                    System.out.println("Database saved. Goodbye!");
                    return;
                }

                default ->
                    System.out.println("Invalid option.");
            }
        }
    }

    private void add(Scanner scanner) {

        System.out.println("\n========== ADD RECORD ==========");

        System.out.print("Name: ");
        String name = scanner.nextLine();

        System.out.print("Information: ");
        String information = scanner.nextLine();

        Record record =
                new Record(nextId++, name, information);

        records.add(record);

        save();

        System.out.println("Record added successfully.");
    }

    private void showAll() {

        System.out.println("\n========== ALL RECORDS ==========");

        if (records.isEmpty()) {
            System.out.println("Database is empty.");
            return;
        }

        records.forEach(Record::display);

        System.out.println("Total records: " + records.size());
    }

    private void search(Scanner scanner) {

        System.out.println("\n========== SEARCH ==========");

        System.out.print("Search: ");
        String query = scanner.nextLine().toLowerCase();

        boolean found = false;

        for (Record record : records) {

            if (record.getName().toLowerCase().contains(query)
                    || record.getInformation()
                            .toLowerCase()
                            .contains(query)) {

                record.display();
                found = true;
            }
        }

        if (!found)
            System.out.println("No matching records.");
    }

    private void edit(Scanner scanner) {

        System.out.print("\nEnter ID: ");

        int id = Integer.parseInt(scanner.nextLine());

        Record record = find(id);

        if (record == null) {
            System.out.println("Record not found.");
            return;
        }

        System.out.print("New name: ");
        String name = scanner.nextLine();

        System.out.print("New information: ");
        String information = scanner.nextLine();

        record.setName(name);
        record.setInformation(information);

        save();

        System.out.println("Record updated.");
    }

    private void delete(Scanner scanner) {

        System.out.print("\nEnter ID: ");

        int id = Integer.parseInt(scanner.nextLine());

        Record record = find(id);

        if (record == null) {
            System.out.println("Record not found.");
            return;
        }

        records.remove(record);

        save();

        System.out.println("Record deleted.");
    }

    private Record find(int id) {

        for (Record record : records) {

            if (record.getId() == id)
                return record;
        }

        return null;
    }

    private void statistics() {

        System.out.println("\n========== STATISTICS ==========");

        System.out.println(
                "Total records : " + records.size());

        System.out.println(
                "Next ID       : " + nextId);

        System.out.println(
                "Storage file  : " + FILE);
    }

    private void save() {

        try (ObjectOutputStream output =
                     new ObjectOutputStream(
                             new FileOutputStream(FILE))) {

            output.writeObject(records);

        } catch (IOException e) {

            System.out.println(
                    "Error saving database: "
                            + e.getMessage());
        }
    }

    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
    private void load() {

        File file = new File(FILE);

        if (!file.exists())
            return;

        try (ObjectInputStream input =
                     new ObjectInputStream(
                             new FileInputStream(FILE))) {

            records.clear();

            records.addAll(
                    (List<Record>) input.readObject());

            for (Record record : records) {

                if (record.getId() >= nextId)
                    nextId = record.getId() + 1;
            }

        } catch (IOException | ClassNotFoundException e) {

            System.out.println(
                    "Could not load database.");
        }
    }
}

This uses classes, objects, inheritance-free OOP design, collections, serialization, file I/O, exception handling, LocalDateTime, lambdas, streams-style operations, and CRUD functionality.
-
Yes, but C is not normally used directly for the front end of a website.

HTML → webpage structure

CSS → design and layout

JavaScript → interaction in the browser

C → can power the backend/server, WebAssembly applications, or high-performance components


For example, a website could be:

Browser
   ↓
HTML + CSS + JavaScript
   ↓
     API
   ↓
C backend/server
   ↓
Database

You can compile C to WebAssembly (Wasm) so C code can run inside a web browser. That's useful for things requiring high performance, such as games, simulations, image/video processing, and scientific calculations.

So if you're building a normal GitHub Pages website, HTML + CSS + JavaScript is the practical choice. C would generally be an additional component rather than replacing them.

Yes — Ada is a real programming language, designed with a strong emphasis on safety, reliability, and correctness, especially for large systems.

Here's a fairly substantial Ada example:

with Ada.Text_IO;
with Ada.Integer_Text_IO;

use Ada.Text_IO;
use Ada.Integer_Text_IO;

procedure Alpha_Database is

   Max_Records : constant := 100;

   type Record is record
      ID   : Integer;
      Name : String (1 .. 30);
      Age  : Integer;
   end record;

   Records : array (1 .. Max_Records) of Record;
   Count   : Integer := 0;

   procedure Add_Record is
      New_Record : Record;
      Input_Name  : String (1 .. 30);
      Last        : Natural;
   begin
      if Count = Max_Records then
         Put_Line ("Database is full.");
         return;
      end if;

      Count := Count + 1;

      New_Record.ID := Count;

      Put ("Name: ");
      Get_Line (Input_Name, Last);

      New_Record.Name := (others => ' ');

      if Last > 0 then
         New_Record.Name (1 .. Last) :=
           Input_Name (1 .. Last);
      end if;

      Put ("Age: ");
      Get (New_Record.Age);

      Records (Count) := New_Record;

      Put_Line ("Record added successfully.");
   end Add_Record;


   procedure Show_Records is
   begin
      Put_Line ("");
      Put_Line ("========== RECORDS ==========");

      if Count = 0 then
         Put_Line ("No records.");
         return;
      end if;

      for I in 1 .. Count loop
         Put ("ID   : ");
         Put (Records (I).ID, Width => 0);

         Put_Line ("");

         Put ("Name : ");
         Put_Line (Records (I).Name);

         Put ("Age  : ");
         Put (Records (I).Age, Width => 0);

         Put_Line ("");
         Put_Line ("-----------------------------");
      end loop;
   end Show_Records;


   procedure Search_Record is
      Search_ID : Integer;
      Found      : Boolean := False;
   begin
      Put ("Enter ID: ");
      Get (Search_ID);

      for I in 1 .. Count loop
         if Records (I).ID = Search_ID then
            Put_Line ("");
            Put_Line ("Record found!");

            Put ("Name: ");
            Put_Line (Records (I).Name);

            Put ("Age: ");
            Put (Records (I).Age, Width => 0);
            Put_Line ("");

            Found := True;
         end if;
      end loop;

      if not Found then
         Put_Line ("Record not found.");
      end if;
   end Search_Record;


   Choice : Integer;

begin

   loop
      Put_Line ("");
      Put_Line ("================================");
      Put_Line ("       ALPHA ADA DATABASE");
      Put_Line ("================================");
      Put_Line ("1. Add record");
      Put_Line ("2. Show records");
      Put_Line ("3. Search");
      Put_Line ("0. Exit");
      Put_Line ("================================");

      Put ("Choose: ");
      Get (Choice);

      case Choice is

         when 1 =>
            Add_Record;

         when 2 =>
            Show_Records;

         when 3 =>
            Search_Record;

         when 0 =>
            Put_Line ("Goodbye!");
            exit;

         when others =>
            Put_Line ("Invalid option.");

      end case;
   end loop;

end Alpha_Database;

Ada is particularly interesting because it has features designed to catch many programming errors before the program runs, including strong typing, range constraints, and contracts.

{
    while (n > 0)
    {
        "%d...\n", n;
        Sleep(1000);
        n--;
    }

    "\nLAUNCH!\n";
}

    "\nStarting countdown:\n\n";

    Countdown(5);

Absolutely. Here's a substantial C# program: a console-based personal database with OOP, LINQ, JSON persistence, searching, sorting, editing, deleting, and statistics.

using System;
using System.Collections.Generic;
using System.IO;
using System.Linq;
using System.Text.Json;

namespace AlphaDatabase
{
    public class Record
    {
        public int Id { get; set; }
        public string Name { get; set; } = "";
        public string Information { get; set; } = "";
        public DateTime Created { get; set; }
    }

    public class Database
    {
        private readonly string fileName = "alpha_database.json";
        private List<Record> records = new();

        public void Load()
        {
            if (!File.Exists(fileName))
                return;

            try
            {
                string json = File.ReadAllText(fileName);

                records = JsonSerializer.Deserialize<List<Record>>(json)
                          ?? new List<Record>();
            }
            catch
            {
                Console.WriteLine("Could not load database.");
                records = new List<Record>();
            }
        }

        public void Save()
        {
            var options = new JsonSerializerOptions
            {
                WriteIndented = true
            };

            string json = JsonSerializer.Serialize(records, options);

            File.WriteAllText(fileName, json);
        }

        private int NextId()
        {
            return records.Count == 0
                ? 1
                : records.Max(r => r.Id) + 1;
        }

        public void Add()
        {
            Console.WriteLine("\n========== ADD RECORD ==========");

            Console.Write("Name: ");
            string name = Console.ReadLine() ?? "";

            Console.Write("Information: ");
            string information = Console.ReadLine() ?? "";

            Record record = new()
            {
                Id = NextId(),
                Name = name,
                Information = information,
                Created = DateTime.Now
            };

            records.Add(record);
            Save();

            Console.WriteLine($"\nRecord #{record.Id} created.");
        }

        public void ShowAll()
        {
            Console.WriteLine("\n========== ALL RECORDS ==========");

            if (!records.Any())
            {
                Console.WriteLine("Database is empty.");
                return;
            }

            foreach (var record in records)
            {
                Display(record);
            }

            Console.WriteLine($"\nTotal records: {records.Count}");
        }

        public void Search()
        {
            Console.WriteLine("\n========== SEARCH ==========");

            Console.Write("Search: ");
            string query = Console.ReadLine() ?? "";

            var results = records
                .Where(r =>
                    r.Name.Contains(query, StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase) ||
                    r.Information.Contains(query, StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase))
                .ToList();

            if (!results.Any())
            {
                Console.WriteLine("No matching records.");
                return;
            }

            foreach (var record in results)
                Display(record);

            Console.WriteLine($"Found {results.Count} record(s).");
        }

        public void Edit()
        {
            Console.WriteLine("\n========== EDIT RECORD ==========");

            Console.Write("Enter ID: ");

            if (!int.TryParse(Console.ReadLine(), out int id))
            {
                Console.WriteLine("Invalid ID.");
                return;
            }

            var record = records.FirstOrDefault(r => r.Id == id);

            if (record == null)
            {
                Console.WriteLine("Record not found.");
                return;
            }

            Console.Write($"New name [{record.Name}]: ");
            string name = Console.ReadLine() ?? "";

            Console.Write($"New information [{record.Information}]: ");
            string information = Console.ReadLine() ?? "";

            if (!string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(name))
                record.Name = name;

            if (!string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(information))
                record.Information = information;

            Save();

            Console.WriteLine("Record updated successfully.");
        }

        public void Delete()
        {
            Console.WriteLine("\n========== DELETE RECORD ==========");

            Console.Write("Enter ID: ");

            if (!int.TryParse(Console.ReadLine(), out int id))
            {
                Console.WriteLine("Invalid ID.");
                return;
            }

            var record = records.FirstOrDefault(r => r.Id == id);

            if (record == null)
            {
                Console.WriteLine("Record not found.");
                return;
            }

            records.Remove(record);
            Save();

            Console.WriteLine("Record deleted successfully.");
        }

        public void Sort()
        {
            records = records
                .OrderBy(r => r.Name)
                .ToList();

            Save();

            Console.WriteLine("\nRecords sorted alphabetically.");
        }

        public void Statistics()
        {
            Console.WriteLine("\n========== STATISTICS ==========");

            Console.WriteLine($"Total records : {records.Count}");

            if (records.Any())
            {
                Console.WriteLine(
                    $"First record  : {records.Min(r => r.Created):yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm:ss}");

                Console.WriteLine(
                    $"Latest record : {records.Max(r => r.Created):yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm:ss}");

                Console.WriteLine(
                    $"Longest name  : {records.Max(r => r.Name.Length)} characters");
            }

            Console.WriteLine($"Storage file  : {Path.GetFullPath(fileName)}");
        }

        private static void Display(Record record)
        {
            Console.WriteLine("----------------------------------------");
            Console.WriteLine($"ID          : {record.Id}");
            Console.WriteLine($"Name        : {record.Name}");
            Console.WriteLine($"Information : {record.Information}");
            Console.WriteLine($"Created     : {record.Created:yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm:ss}");
            Console.WriteLine("----------------------------------------");
        }
    }

    internal class Program
    {
        static void Main()
        {
            Database database = new();

            database.Load();

            Console.WriteLine("========================================");
            Console.WriteLine("       ALPHA C# DATABASE SYSTEM");
            Console.WriteLine("========================================");

            while (true)
            {
                Console.WriteLine(@"
1. Add record
2. Show all records
3. Search
4. Edit
5. Delete
6. Sort by name
7. Statistics
8. Save
0. Exit
");

                Console.Write("Choose: ");

                string choice = Console.ReadLine() ?? "";

                switch (choice)
                {
                    case "1":
                        database.Add();
                        break;

                    case "2":
                        database.ShowAll();
                        break;

                    case "3":
                        database.Search();
                        break;

                    case "4":
                        database.Edit();
                        break;

                    case "5":
                        database.Delete();
                        break;

                    case "6":
                        database.Sort();
                        break;

                    case "7":
                        database.Statistics();
                        break;

                    case "8":
                        database.Save();
                        Console.WriteLine("Database saved.");
                        break;

                    case "0":
                        database.Save();
                        Console.WriteLine("Database saved. Goodbye!");
                        return;

                    default:
                        Console.WriteLine("Invalid option.");
                        break;
                }
            }
        }
    }
}

This one demonstrates classes, objects, properties, collections, LINQ, JSON serialization, file I/O, exception handling, generics, lambda expressions, DateTime, and CRUD operations—much closer to real-world C# than a simple beginner example.
-
The Commit at 03:17

At exactly 03:17:42 AM, the repository changed.

Nobody was supposed to be awake.

The GitHub repository belonged to a small group of university students who had built an offline emergency-information system. The project had been abandoned for almost eight months.

No one had pushed code.

No one had access to the organization anymore.

At least, that was what everyone believed.

---

03:18 AM

Arif was the first to notice.

He was awake because his laptop was running a backup script.

A notification appeared:

«New commit pushed to "main".»

He stared at the screen.

The commit message was:

«"restore"»

There was only one changed file:

"archive/README.txt"

Arif opened it.

The file contained a single sentence:

«“You deleted the wrong copy.”»

He assumed somebody had hacked the repository.

He checked the contributors.

Nothing.

The commit appeared under an old account that had been inactive for nearly a year.

Arif immediately messaged the others.

Nobody answered.

---

03:31 AM

He downloaded the repository.

The strange file wasn't actually new.

Git showed something impossible.

The file had existed in an older commit from eight months earlier.

But its contents were different.

Arif opened the Git history.

Commit after commit.

Month after month.

Then he noticed something strange.

Every version of the file had exactly one extra character.

A letter.

Then another.

Then another.

He copied the characters into a text editor.

They formed:

«CHECK THE CLOCK»

Arif looked at his computer.

03:31.

Nothing happened.

Then his phone vibrated.

A notification from GitHub.

Another commit.

---

03:32 AM

The commit message was:

«"03:32"»

This time, the changed file was:

"archive/time.txt"

It contained:

«03:17:42 — first signal
03:18:09 — second signal
03:31:54 — observer detected»

Arif stopped breathing for a moment.

Observer detected?

He checked the repository's traffic logs.

There was one visitor.

The visitor had accessed the repository at exactly:

03:31:54 AM.

But the visitor wasn't him.

The IP address belonged to the university network.

---

03:36 AM

Arif called his friend Sami.

Sami answered after the fourth ring.

“Did you see it?”

“Yes.”

“What do you think it is?”

“I don't know.”

There was silence.

Then Sami said:

“Arif... I'm looking at the old server.”

“What server?”

“The one we used before we moved everything to GitHub.”

“That server was destroyed.”

“I know.”

Another silence.

“It's online.”

---

03:41 AM

They connected to it.

The machine had no normal operating system interface.

Only a black terminal.

One line appeared:

SYSTEM RESTORED

Then:

LAST HUMAN ACCESS: 2025-12-14 03:17:42 UTC

Arif frowned.

“That date…”

Sami whispered:

“That's the night we abandoned the project.”

A new line appeared.

ARCHIVE STATUS: INCOMPLETE

Then:

MISSING USER: ARIF

Arif moved away from the laptop.

“That's not funny.”

“I didn't type anything.”

The terminal continued.

MISSING USER: SAMI

Then:

MISSING USER: NABIL

Their three names.

Exactly the three people who had originally created the system.

---

03:47 AM

The server displayed one final message.

DO YOU REMEMBER WHY YOU STOPPED?

Neither answered.

Because they did remember.

Eight months earlier, they had discovered an unexplained folder inside their archive.

It contained information nobody remembered creating.

Photographs.

System logs.

Maps.

Audio recordings.

And thousands of small text files.

Every file had a future timestamp.

They had become frightened.

So they deleted everything.

Or thought they did.

Sami suddenly opened the GitHub repository again.

The newest commit had appeared.

The message was:

«"we never deleted it"»

Underneath was a file:

"/archive/03-17/recording.mp4"

They played it.

At first, there was only static.

Then their own voices appeared.

Recorded eight months earlier.

Arif heard himself say:

«“If you're watching this, don't trust the repository.”»

Sami looked at him.

“That recording wasn't there before.”

The video continued.

Their younger selves were sitting in the same room.

But something was different.

Someone else was standing behind them.

A person neither of them recognized.

The recording ended.

The final frame showed a timestamp:

2026-10-01 03:17:42

Arif checked the date.

October 1st.

Today.

He checked the time.

03:49 AM.

The timestamp was from the future.

Except it wasn't.

The recording had been created eight months ago.

---

03:52 AM

A final GitHub notification appeared.

This time, there was no commit message.

Only:

«"main" was updated.»

Arif opened the repository.

The entire project had changed.

The README now contained a new line:

«“Thank you for finishing the archive.”»

Then the repository disappeared.

Not deleted.

Not private.

Just...

gone.

The URL returned a 404.

Sami refreshed.

Again.

404. 

Then Arif's phone vibrated.

One final notification.

Not from GitHub.

From the old server.

ARCHIVE COMPLETE

A second line appeared.

NEXT OBSERVER: ARIF

And beneath it:

03:17:42 — TOMORROW

Arif slowly looked at the clock.

03:53 AM.

For the first time that night, he realized something.

The system had never been trying to contact them.

It had been waiting for them to discover it.

And whatever created the archive...

had known exactly when they would.
